## core/valet.rs / repo.rs / wordpress.rs / devtools.rs / mail.rs / ports.rs / ssl.rs

| # | Anchor | Claim | Verdict |
|---|---|---|---|
| 132 | valet.rs:3 | Scan writes nothing, anywhere | ◐ surfaced-not-dropped ✅; absolute write-nothing ✅ live (`valet_scan_check` fingerprints) |
| 133 | valet.rs:14 | No site silently dropped | ✅ lib test + `valet_scan_check` |
| 423 | core/terminal.rs (`login_args`, the spawn) + examples/terminal_check.rs | **The built-in terminal spawns a LOGIN shell — otherwise it is a terminal that cannot run the developer's tools.** rexenv is launched by launchd, so its PATH is the bare `/usr/bin:/bin:/usr/sbin:/sbin`, and a non-login zsh reads only `~/.zshrc`: never `/etc/zprofile` (path_helper → `/etc/paths`) nor `~/.zprofile` (`brew shellenv`). `code`, `rex`, brew git and every version-manager shim answered `command not found` inside rexenv while working in Terminal.app (which runs `login -pf`) — **reported from the app's own terminal 27 Aug 2026, not found by review**. Unknown shells get NO flag: a bad flag fails the spawn, and a short PATH beats a terminal that will not open. The bundled-PHP prepend still wins, because the injected `export` runs AFTER rc | ✅ `known_shells_are_launched_as_login_shells` (L0) + `terminal_check` (L1, sandbox tier). **The example's own PATH was the fixture that hid this**: it inherited a developer shell's PATH, under which a non-login shell passes every assertion. It now shrinks PATH to the launchd four BEFORE opening the session and requires the shell to echo back a PATH containing `/usr/local/bin` — a dir only path_helper, i.e. only a login shell, can restore. **Not covered:** shells outside the recognised list, and the startup COST — a real `~/.zshrc` takes seconds, which is why the deadlines are 30s and a slow rc will look like a slow terminal |
| 424 | components/terminal/SiteTerminal.tsx (`live`, `parkingBay`, `MAX_LIVE`) | **A terminal session outlives the tab that shows it — and the DOM node is moved, never rebuilt.** Reported 27 Aug 2026: leave the Terminal tab, come back, and the shell had been killed and the scrollback disposed, so a long `composer install` died on an alt-tab with its output gone. Sessions now live in a module map keyed by site. Two mechanics are load-bearing and neither is obvious: xterm cannot be `open()`ed twice, so the container is MOVED; and it is parked in an offscreen holder INSIDE the document rather than detached, because a detached node measures 0×0 and the renderer sizes itself from that. Bounded at 4 live shells — each is a full login shell (#423) — and an evicted one reopens on the next visit, which is what every session did before | 🔨 **L2** — a harness route can mount the tab, run a command, unmount, remount and assert the earlier output is still on screen and the PTY id unchanged. Not provable at L0 (no DOM, no PTY) and not at L1 (the store is frontend). Until then it rests on the user's own repeat of the reported flow: `docs/SMOKE-TEST.md` |
| 425 | core/terminal.rs (`asset_cwd`) + commands/terminal.rs (`AssetRef`) | **A terminal opened from a plugin/theme row starts in THAT folder, resolved here — the frontend never names a path, and an asset with no folder of its own is an error rather than a shell somewhere else.** The directory comes from `repo::asset_dest`, which is already the ONE place the recorded content dir (`app` for Bedrock, `content` for Radicle) and the folder-name validation live; a second join of `wp-content/plugins/<name>` here would have opened Bedrock shells in a path that does not exist and would have taken the name straight from IPC (the M7 class). The refusal is the honest half: a single-file plugin (`hello.php`) and a drop-in have no folder, and a silent fall back to the docroot would have read as *this IS the plugin's directory*. Rows that can only be folderless (must-use, drop-in) do not show the button at all | ✅ `asset_cwd_follows_the_recorded_content_dir_and_refuses_a_folderless_asset` — a real Bedrock-shaped tree on disk (`app/plugins/…`), the folderless refusal, and both validations still firing through `asset_dest` (bad kind, `../../etc`) |
| 134 | repo.rs:11 | No frozen spinner; prompts fail fast; argv validated at parse | ✅ 4 lib tests + `repo_clone_check` |
| 135 | repo.rs:756 | Clone refuses existing dir; submodules never processed | ✅ lib test |
| 136 | repo.rs:1157 | Never merge/rebase/force for the user; `--prune-tags` never | ✅ 2 lib tests + `repo_git_ops_check` |
| 310 | repo.rs (`stash_push_args`, `git_stash_push`) | **A stash never takes IGNORED paths, and never reports a stash that isn't there.** `-u` is deliberate (untracked in, so the tree is genuinely clean and the checkout that prompted this can't still be blocked); `-a` is the flag that must never appear — under `.gitignore` here live `vendor/` and `node_modules/`, so `--all` would park minutes of install time in an entry that then conflicts with the next install, and the user's own build output would vanish from a button labelled "Stash". Second half: git's "No local changes to save" exits 0, so a clean tree is refused BEFORE running rather than passing as a stash the restore list can't show | ✅ `stash_takes_untracked_but_never_ignored_files` (argv, both directions) + L1 `repo_git_ops_check` legs 10/14 — an ignored `vendor/autoload.php` is asserted still on disk AFTER the stash (the `-a` regression passes every other assertion in that leg), and a clean tree refuses by name |
| 311 | repo.rs (`reset_hard_args`, `git_reset_hard`) + RepoPanel confirm | **Reset reverts tracked changes and DELETES NOTHING ELSE.** `git reset --hard HEAD`, no pathspec, and explicitly no `git clean`: a file the user wrote five minutes ago and never added survives an action whose name sounds like it takes everything — and it must, because unlike the tracked half there is no stash, no reflog and no remote to bring it back from. The confirm dialog states both halves with the real counts ("N changed files revert and cannot be recovered; M untracked files are kept"), so the promise and the argv are one claim | ✅ `reset_is_the_whole_tree_and_never_deletes_untracked_files` (argv carries no clean/-f/-d) + L1 `repo_git_ops_check` leg 13 — after a real reset the tracked edit is gone, the untracked file is still there, and the ignored path is untouched. ◐ the CONFIRM's counts are L2 (`repopanel.js` dirty scenario); the dialog wording itself is a copy guard, today a review rule |
| 312 | repo.rs (`validate_stash_ref`, `parse_stash_list`) + commands/repo.rs (`repo_git_op` "stash-pop" arm, `repo_stashes`) | **A stash entry is a REVISION, and only `stash@{N}` ever reaches pop.** Revision syntax is an expression language — `stash@{0}^{/text}`, `HEAD@{now}`, `:/text` all resolve, and pop acts on whatever it is handed — so the check is a whitelist of the one shape the UI produces rather than [`validate_ref`] with braces added. The list is read LIVE on every open and never cached in our own state: git renumbers on every pop, so a held `stash@{1}` silently names a different entry than the row the user is looking at, and a line that doesn't parse as three fields is DROPPED rather than guessed at | ✅ `a_stash_ref_is_exactly_stash_at_n_and_nothing_else` (8 refused shapes) + `the_stash_list_parses_messages_with_separators_in_them` (unit-separated, so a subject containing colons/spaces can't fake a field) + L1 `repo_git_ops_check` leg 15 — four revision expressions refused before git runs, and a stale index maps to "the list renumbers on every restore" rather than a raw git error |

A guard asserting the four would have passed while two unpinned spawns ran a user's packages: a guard narrower than its own claim, the family that has cost this project four times in a month. (b) The pinned value is a **FILE** beside the phar, not an empty directory: an empty dir is neutral today and populated by the first `wp package install` an agent sends through the raw runner, after which the pin is gone with every test still green. A file cannot contain `vendor/autoload.php`, so the pin holds by the shape of the path rather than by nobody writing there. Measured against the pinned phar 2.12.0: ordinary commands unaffected and silent, a package command answers `not a registered wp command`, `wp package list` refuses loudly with `mkdir(): File exists`. The scan strips comments and carries `the_scan_reads_code_and_not_its_own_comments` in both directions, because the #235 copy guard shipped defective by reading its own explanation and **this one did too on first run** — it counted the two `Command::new(php_bin)` mentions in its own prose and failed at 3. **The L1 plants its own canary**: a fixture `vendor/autoload.php` registering `rexenv-canary` at `before_wp_load`, and leg A REQUIRES it to resolve when WP-CLI is pointed there — if it does not, the example reports **CONTROL LEG BROKEN, this run proves NOTHING** and exits, rather than reporting the pin as proven off a command that was missing for its own reasons (the vacuous shape one layer up; #236's lesson applied before it could bite). With that dir then EXPORTED into the process, legs B and C require `not a registered wp command` through both production spawns, D requires the terminal wrapper to still resolve it, E requires a freshly created pin to be a file. **Plant-proven five ways**, each failing alone: a canary without `before_wp_load` → broken control; the pin removed from `wp_command` → B resolved it; from `wp_step_streamed` → C resolved it; the wrapper pinned → D; `create_dir_all` instead of the file → E. **E's first version passed that last plant** — it read the pin file an earlier run had left beside the real phar, so it observed sticky state instead of exercising creation; it now does both. **SCOPE — what the green run showed, not what the claim hopes.** It showed that on this machine, under the real pinned phar, a package registering via `WP_CLI::add_command` does not resolve through either production path while the user's own `WP_CLI_PACKAGES_DIR` is exported. Mark all read patches for the same reason and deliberately does NOT invalidate: the write succeeded, so the patch IS the truth, and an immediate refetch only opens a window for an in-flight list to answer with the pre-write state — a button that worked, reading as one that didn't | ✅ L2 `mail.js` — the opened row flips with the list-call count UNCHANGED across the click (so a refetch cannot be what did it), and Mark all read flips the screen under `stale=1`, where the mocked server keeps reporting the messages unread. **Plant-proven**: removing the patch fails by name |
| 314 | core/mail.rs (`search_query`, `UNREAD_QUERY`) + commands/mail.rs (`mailpit_messages`) | **The unread filter is a Mailpit SEARCH, and it narrows the user's search instead of replacing it.** Filtering the returned page in the UI would only hide the read messages that made it onto that page, leaving the unread ones that didn't just as unfindable — the exact problem the filter exists for. And the two terms compose in ONE place, because a filter that replaced the search would widen the list at the moment the user was narrowing it, while still looking correct (unread mail did appear). `unread_only` therefore crosses IPC as a flag, never as text the frontend splices into the query | ✅ `the_unread_filter_narrows_a_search_instead_of_replacing_it` (7 compositions incl. whitespace-only search) + L1 `mail_api_check` (run AFTER a preview, so exactly one of two messages is unread — on a fresh or swept inbox a filter that returns everything would pass) + L2 `mail.js` asserts `unreadOnly` on the wire. **Plant-proven**: filtering client-side fails by name |
| 315 | core/mail.rs (`mark_all_read`) + routes/Mail.tsx (the pinned row) | **"Mark all read" flips a flag and nothing else, and the message you are READING never disappears out from under you.** The empty-body shape is Mailpit's "all mailbox messages" here and its "delete everything" on the DELETE endpoint one function away — which is why [`delete`] refuses an empty ID list and why marking everything read is spelled as its own function with no id-taking variant to pass empty by accident. The second half is the filter's own trap: opening a message makes it read, so under `is:unread` the next poll drops it and the open preview goes with it — the filter would punish the user for using it. The row stays pinned while selected, and only while the FILTER is what removed it (a deleted message still vanishes) | ✅ L1 `mail_api_check` (unread → 0, every message read, and `total` asserted UNCHANGED — a "mark read" that deleted messages would otherwise pass) + L2 `mail.js` (the read message survives a full 5s poll under the filter with its preview intact). **Plant-proven**: un-pinning the open row fails by name |

## core/laravel.rs + the served-root record (v32)

| # | Anchor | Claim | Verdict |
|---|---|---|---|
| 244 | state/models.rs (`Site::served_root`, `docroot_subdir`) + core/sites.rs (`LARAVEL_DOCROOT_SUBDIR`, the create rule) + state/db.rs v32 | **The folder we SERVE and the folder that IS the site are two facts, combined in exactly one place.** A Laravel project's front controller is `public/index.php` and its `.env` — the site's database credentials — sits one level ABOVE it, so serving the project root would put that file on a public URL. `path` keeps meaning the project root (what teardown removes, where composer/artisan run); `docroot_subdir` says what to append; `served_root()` is the only combiner, called by the nginx vhost, the FrankenPHP/Apache override and the desired-state map — three call sites that would otherwise each be an opportunity to get it right twice and wrong once. The subdir is recorded at create ONLY for a docroot rexenv OWNS: a LINKED project's stored path already points at the folder to serve (`docroot_rel` was applied at link time), so appending `public` there would serve a directory that does not exist. v32 backfills on that same rule, because Laravel rows created before the install flow hold the column default and would start serving a project root the day a real app landed in one | ✅ 2 lib tests — `served_root_is_the_subdir_and_never_the_project_root_that_holds_dot_env` and `v32_backfills_public_only_for_laravel_docroots_rexenv_owns` (all four ownership/type cases pinned, because being wrong in either direction is its own bug: too eager serves a missing folder, too shy publishes `.env`). **The wire-level half is inherited, not re-proven here**: that a request for `/.env` inside a served root 404s is #103's dotfile guard (nginx leg ✅ `dotfile_guard_check`) — this row is about `.env` never being inside the served root in the first place, which is the defence that does not depend on it |
| 245 | core/laravel.rs (`wire_env`, `set_env_key`) | **One answer per key in the generated `.env`, whatever shape the skeleton shipped.** `composer create-project laravel/laravel` writes `DB_CONNECTION=sqlite` live with the MySQL keys COMMENTED OUT (measured on laravel/framework ^13.8), and older skeletons ship them live with placeholder values. A writer that appends would leave `# DB_DATABASE=laravel` above `DB_DATABASE=wp_x_rex` — two answers, with dotenv readers disagreeing about which wins — so a commented entry is replaced IN PLACE and duplicates are dropped rather than left below. Deliberately NOT `core::confedit`'s refuse-rather-than-guess machinery: this is a file we generated seconds earlier, not a user's hand-edited config, and widening confedit's closed key set would have destroyed its own property (a password change is unrepresentable there, D1) | ✅ 4 lib tests. The fixture is the REAL generated file, copied verbatim from a live `create-project` run rather than invented — an invented one would not have had the commented block at all, and that block is the half that decides whether the app talks to this site's database or to a SQLite file nobody can see (the friendly-fixture family). A 5th test re-runs the writer over a genuinely generated `.env` when one is staged (`REXENV_LARAVEL_DOTENV`). **Amended 21 Aug 2026 — that last clause used to read "so the day the skeleton changes shape the hand-copied fixture cannot go stale in silence", and it can.** Nothing sets that variable: a repo-wide search finds the test and this row, and no example runs `composer create-project` (the clone-based checks read the repo's `.env.example`, which is a different file). So the 5th test skips on every run, silently — the guard against silence is itself silent. It is a MANUAL leg now and says so: `REXENV_LARAVEL_DOTENV=<path-to-a-real-generated-.env> cargo test --lib wire_env_over_a_real`. The four fixture tests are what actually holds, and they hold against a copy that CAN go stale |
| 246 | commands/site_provision.rs (the Laravel `configure` phase) | **The migrations the site advertises ran against the database the site advertises.** The skeleton's own post-create script runs `artisan migrate --graceful` while `.env` still says sqlite, so `users`/`cache`/`jobs` land in `database/database.sqlite` and the site's MySQL database is EMPTY — a site whose Databases screen shows a database the app never filled. `configure` therefore re-runs the migrations AFTER wiring `.env`. The stray SQLite file is deliberately left alone (it is the project's own file; deleting what Composer wrote would be a surprise) — it is simply no longer what `.env` names | 🔨 L1 (an example creating a Laravel site and asserting the site's MySQL database holds the migration tables — today the ordering is verified by the live run that FOUND it, 9 Aug 2026, and by nothing automated) |
| 247 | commands/site_provision.rs (`ensure_blueprint_applies`) | **A blueprint is refused wherever no blueprint phase would run — never accepted and silently ignored.** Blueprints are a WordPress preset (plugins, themes, multisite mode, WP_DEBUG, language) and `phase_defs` gives the blueprint phase only to a MANAGED WordPress site. Passing one for a Laravel or Blank-PHP site, or for a linked folder rexenv adopts as-is, used to be accepted, carried through the whole job, and do nothing — succeeding while doing nothing is the dishonest half. Refused BEFORE the row is inserted, so the caller gets a prepare-phase error with nothing created rather than a half-site; refused at the one choke point every create path funnels through (UI, CLI, MCP), so a caller that never sees a dialog is told the same thing | ✅ 2 lib tests, one of which pins the two functions against each other: the shapes the guard ADMITS are exactly the shapes `phase_defs` gives a blueprint phase to. That drift is what made this a silent no-op rather than an error, so it is the assertion, not the guard alone |
| 248 | src/components/sites/SiteProvisionCard.tsx (the header row) + scripts/wk-checks/uireview.js (`provisionRow`) | **A backend phase label can never push the domain out of the card.** Phase labels are backend copy varying in length by a factor of three, and the header row held the domain, the label and both buttons with the label `flex-none` — at the New Site dialog's width "installing Laravel (composer create-project)" overflowed the row and left nothing of "creating &lt;domain&gt;", the one thing the card is about. The label now renders on the detail line below; the header has a FIXED cast (domain, Cancel, log) so no future label competes with it | ✅ L2 — `/dev/ui-review?view=provision` renders the card at the dialog's own 420px with the longest real label, and the probe asserts the domain keeps readable width, nothing wraps, and nothing crosses the card's right edge. **The probe failed the FIRST fix** (domain squeezed to 59px by a shrink-and-truncate label), which is how the header-row approach was ruled out rather than shipped — by eye it looked like correct truncation |

| 429 | core/starter.rs (`seed`) | **Seeding is idempotent: a retry adds no rows and destroys none.** `CREATE TABLE IF NOT EXISTS` + a seed guarded by `WHERE NOT EXISTS`, rather than DROP-and-recreate, which would delete whatever the developer had put in the table by the time they pressed Retry | ✅ `starter_seed_check` (L1 sandbox tier) — its own mysqld on a fixture port: seed → 4 rows, seed again → still 4, then a row the DEVELOPER inserted survives a third seed (5 rows, `mine=1`). ## core/wp_dns.rs (loopback DNS for PHP — 10 Aug 2026)

| # | Anchor | Claim | Verdict |
|---|---|---|---|
| 251 | core/wp_dns.rs (module doc + `MU_PLUGIN`) | **A WordPress site can reach its OWN hostname from php-fpm.** The bundled static-php builds link libcurl against c-ares (`curl_version()['ares'] = 1.34.6`), which reads `/etc/resolv.conf` alone and never macOS split-DNS — so `gethostbyname("x.rex")` answered `127.0.0.1` while cURL to the same host returned errno 6, and WP-Cron (fire-and-forget, result never checked) stopped on every hosted site in total silence. The mu-plugin hands cURL the system resolver's own answer via `CURLOPT_RESOLVE` on `http_api_curl` | ✅ `wp_dns_check` — runs the REAL plugin source under the REAL bundled PHP against a fixture listener and asserts BOTH halves: unaided cURL fails with errno 6 (the bug still reproduces on this build) and the same request returns 200 with the plugin. A test that only proved the fix would go green the day the bug quietly changed shape |
| 252 | core/wp_dns.rs (`rexenv_dns_resolver_is_loopback`) | **Only zones served from THIS machine are redirected.** The plugin acts on a host only when `/etc/resolver/<tld>` exists AND names a loopback nameserver (`127.x` / `::1`); a company VPN's split-DNS (`nameserver 10.8.0.1`) and every public domain fall through untouched. Rerouting those to whatever the local resolver says would break a working setup in the name of fixing a local one — and the TLD is concatenated into a filesystem path, so it is `[a-z0-9-]{1,63}`-checked first | ✅ `wp_dns_check` — the predicate is a separate PHP function precisely so the NEGATIVE cases are provable without root: ours (v4 + v6) true; VPN, LAN and empty resolver files false; a public host resolves to `null` (no resolver file at all) |
| 253 | core/wp_dns.rs (`ensure_for_site`, `ensure_all`) + commands/site_provision.rs (settle-ok) + commands/sites.rs (rename) + lib.rs (launch) | **Every WordPress site rexenv hosts has the file, and it never goes stale.** Nothing per-site is baked in (no domain, no TLD list), so a domain change or a new TLD needs no rewrite — but the mu-plugin SWEEP removes it at rename, so the rename path re-installs immediately; otherwise Change domain would silently un-fix cron until the next launch. Installed at the one path every create takes; the launch pass covers sites that predate the fix or lost the file | ◐ the writer is proven at L0 (idempotence, tamper-restore, every-layout removal, no placeholder) and the whole-fleet pass is plain code over `sites::list`; that each of the four call sites actually runs is 🔨 L1 (an example creating a site and asserting the file exists in its docroot) |

The claims are bounded by what is actually known: an INSTALL toasts from the settled job — including `partial` as its own outcome, because three slugs with one bad is neither success nor failure — and the start of one says nothing at all; an UPDATE toasts only on the success path, where wp-cli's non-zero exit for any failed item means the count is safe to state | ✅ `wk-checks/wptoast.js` covers all five: Activate/Deactivate (once each, verb + name), Delete (nothing on opening the dialog, the toast only after confirming), Update (a count on success; on `?update=fail` wp-cli's own "Only updated 0 of 1" and never a cheerful count), and Install ok/partial/running — `partial` asserted to keep its own wording and wp-cli's summary rather than rounding to either side, and a RUNNING job asserted to say nothing. | 249 | core/wordpress.rs (`UpdateKind`, `UpdateTracker`, `UPDATE_STEPS`, `update_streamed`) + commands/wordpress.rs (`run_update_streamed`, `wp_plugin_update`/`wp_theme_update`/`wp_core_update`) + WordPressManager.tsx (`useUpdateStream`, `ProgressBar`) | **An update bar reports WP-CLI's own steps — it never runs on a clock, and never claims a byte percentage WP-CLI does not report.** Added 9 Aug 2026 for plugins, extended the same day to themes and core: each was one silent wp-cli call, so WooCommerce, Elementor or a core release gave tens of seconds of a dead button and then a vanished row. Every field is sourced: `done` counts items WP-CLI announced as settled (a FAILED item counts and is LABELLED `Failed` — the run moved on, but it did not succeed), `phase` is the upgrader's own line, and `fraction` = settled items + the current item's STEP POSITION in a fixed, WP-authored sequence. A line that matches nothing (a table row, a warning) is logged and moves nothing, so noise can never advance the bar. Rows WP-CLI has not reached read `Queued` rather than showing a bar at zero as if work were happening. The current item is the argv-order name, refined by the slug in a wp.org download URL only when it parses as a slug — a premium plugin downloading from its own host keeps the argv name instead of showing a filename, and core (whose archive is named for a VERSION) reads no slug at all. **ONE parser across three nouns, not three**: it is WP's single `WP_Upgrader` wearing a noun, so only the settled line differs per kind — three copies would have been three places for the bar to start lying | ✅ 9 lib tests over transcripts whose every phrase was checked against BOTH ends of the pipeline rather than remembered — WP core's upgrader string tables (`class-wp-upgrader.php`, `class-plugin-upgrader.php`, `class-theme-upgrader.php`, `class-language-pack-upgrader.php`) for the phrases, and the pinned wp-cli 2.12.0 phar for the `strip_tags` + `&#8230;`→`...` transform that decides what a line actually looks like, plus wp-cli's OWN lines (`Using cached file '%s'...`, `Updating to version %s (%s)...`, `Cleaning up files...`, `Success: WordPress updated successfully.`). **Reading the sources found a bug the tidy fixture had hidden**: WP runs a LANGUAGE-PACK upgrade inside a plugin/theme update, and its `Translation updated successfully.` was being counted as an item finishing — one phantom item banked per translation pass, the bar running ahead of the work and reaching 100% with plugins still updating. It is a STEP now, asserted as never-a-settle. **Two faults from the FIRST real use, both in the wiring the tests do not reach** (fixed same day): (a) the bar sat at 100% "Updated" for seconds before vanishing — `onSuccess: onDone` where the caller's arrow implicitly returned `qc.invalidateQueries()`, and react-query AWAITS a promise returned from that callback, so the mutation stayed pending for the whole slow wp.org re-check; the hook now discards the return so no caller can block the bar by accident. (b) the update badge and arrow came BACK for a few seconds after a successful update — invalidating a query refetches but does not erase, so the checked pass's stale row kept being merged over the fresh list; the updated names are now dropped from that cache, falling back to the fast list's own truth until the real answer lands. Both are the same lesson as the row's ◐: **the parser was the tested half and neither fault lived there.** **Scope, stated: the PARSER is proven, the WIRING is not** — that the emit reaches the panel and a bar advances on a real download is 🔨 L2/L3, unproven at any layer today (`docs/TODO.md`)   **L2 30 Aug 2026 — the WIRE is now proven, and it had never been exercised at any layer**: `wk-checks/wpupdate.js` (A) starts a real two-plugin run through `PluginsPanel`, emits `wp-update://plugins/dev` payloads the way the backend does, and reads the RENDERED bar — 0 → 25 → 60, with the phase word, the `n of m` counter and the plugin wp-cli is sitting on following the stream, and the bar CLEARING when the run ends. Plant-proven by severing the listener (`onWpUpdate(…, () => {})`): five assertions fail, the bar sits at 0 and the phase stays "Starting". **What unlocked it was the harness, not the claim**: `DevGitPanel` answered `plugin:event|listen` with a bare `1`, so every `listen()` in the app resolved and could never fire — no streamed UI had ever rendered under WebKit. `mockIPC(…, { shouldMockEvents: true })` turns that on for update progress, repo jobs, installs and provision alike. The row said this needed a real site for weeks; it needed a listener that could fire |

| 250 | WordPressManager.tsx (`verdict`, `isNewerVersion`, `settleAfterUpdate`, `useWpPlugins`/`useWpThemes`) | **A row may claim an update ONLY when the offered version is newer than the one on disk, and a finished update settles both caches before it refetches.** Added 9 Aug 2026, after the badge and the arrow survived a finished update by MINUTES on a real site — the second and worse half of #249's wiring faults, and the first fix (drop the checked row) was aimed at seconds when the fault was measured in minutes. Two independent causes, so two guards. TIMING: the checked pass on a loaded site takes tens of seconds to over a minute (every plugin against wp.org, plus every premium plugin's own API), so a check that started BEFORE the update is normally still in flight when the update ends, and its answer — describing the old disk — lands on top of the drop and restores the badge until the NEXT check finishes. `settleAfterUpdate` cancels that check FIRST, erases what BOTH caches say about the updated names (wp-cli exited 0: they are at the version it installed), then invalidates, so the refetch is the only writer left. TRUTH: `--skip-update-check` does not mean *no update info*, it means *read the update transient without refreshing it* — so both passes hand up claims that can be stale, and `verdict` is the ONE place either list decides one, refusing any claim whose target is not NEWER than the installed version. That makes "update to X" over an item already at X unrenderable whatever the source — in-flight check, stale transient, or a premium plugin's own updater caching its answer for hours. Compared segment-by-numeric-segment because a string compare calls `1.1.11` older than `1.1.3.8`; a non-numeric segment (`1.2.0-beta1`) can't be ordered, so any difference counts as newer — the conservative answer for an offer. Core is the same fault in one item: its update now invalidates `wp-info`, the source of the core version every other card shows | 🔨 L2 — the BACKEND half was measured before writing this (wp-cli 2.12.0 + WP 6.x on a real site: `plugin update` wipes the `update_plugins` transient, so the fast pass after an update is honest, and a checked pass after it is honest too — i.e. **no backend layer produces the stale claim; every one of them is a frontend cache/timing artefact**, which is why the fix is entirely in the panel). ✅ **the rule is held where it RENDERS since 13 Aug 2026** — `wpverdict.js`, over two fixture rows in `DevGitPanel`'s `plugins=list`, because the rule is a pure function and this repo has no JS test runner to hold one; adding a runner for two assertions costs more than proving them through the real component. **Both directions, each failing differently**: `stale-claim` (on 3.4.1, offered 3.4.1) must render NO badge, NO icon-button and NO `→` — the stale-source claim that made a finished update's badge come back; `numeric-order` (on 1.1.3.8, offered 1.1.11) MUST render all three, which is the direction a naive `next > have` breaks and a check testing only the first case would let through. **Plant-proven both ways**, the string-compare plant being the instructive one since it is the fix someone reaches for. A third row (`wordpress-seo`, the ordinary case) is asserted to still offer, so the two above cannot pass on a panel where nothing ever offers anything, and every row is proven PRESENT first — "no badge" is also what an empty list looks like. **Correction recorded**: the plan said `uireview.js` drives the panel over mock data; it does not — `DevGitPanel` + `wptoast.js` do, and the probe went there instead. The probe's own first version also reported the WORKING rows as broken, because the Update control is icon-only (`ArrowUpCircle`) and its version lives in the `title`, so matching on button text found nothing and read as "no offer" everywhere. The TIMING half (cancel-then-settle beats an in-flight check) still needs a real site — same run as #249   **L2 30 Aug 2026 — held, and the row's own attribution was wrong**: `wk-checks/wpupdate.js` (B) arms a late, STALE plugin-list response (`available` with an EMPTY target — the shape `verdict` lets through by design, since it cannot be ordered), puts it in flight, finishes a real update on that row, and asserts the badge does not come back; a neighbouring row that should still offer 22.4 is the anti-vacuity guard. **The first version of this check passed a plant and therefore proved nothing.** Measuring the four combinations showed `settleAfterUpdate` is REDUNDANT BY TWO — cancel-only keeps the badge off, invalidate-only keeps it off (react-query drops a resolution superseded by a newer fetch), and only removing BOTH brings it back. So the guard is the pair, not `cancelQueries` alone, and the redundancy is load-bearing in one direction: the invalidate refetches only while something observes the query, so the cancel is what holds when nothing does. The check holds the composite, user-visible claim and says in its own comments that it cannot attribute further |

Recomputed mechanically with the one-liner above. The working backlog = every 🔨
row + the noted half of every ◐ row, ranked below.

**A STALE INDEX OVER ACCURATE ENTRIES is the dangerous shape** — worse than an
incomplete one, because it reads as a decision rather than a gap. 13 Aug 2026: the
highest-risk cluster below still said #103 was "proven only as a substring… never a
live 404" long after `dotfile_guard_check` proved the nginx leg over the wire. The row
was right and current; the summary was old; the summary is what got read, and the next
piece of work was aimed at building something that already existed. Same fix as the
tally: **anything that restates the ledger is generated from it or points at it, never
paraphrases it.** Until the cluster list below is generated, it is corrected in the
same commit as any row it names.

**The tally was STALE when this was written, and it is now GENERATED rather than
typed.** It read "✅ 139 · ◐ 39 · 🔨 37 · 🚫 5 of 220" — the 3 Aug figures —
while #221–#227 had already landed: seven rows, plus one ◐ upgraded to ✅, all
inside a single day's work. The maintenance rule ("same commit as the comment")
was followed for the ROWS and skipped for the SUMMARY, which is the one part of
a 500-line file anyone reads at a glance.

Telling people to recompute was already the rule, and the rule is what failed —
so `scripts/ledger-tally.sh` now computes the line, and `verify.sh` **fails the
build when this file disagrees with it**. A stale tally is no longer possible to
commit. It also refuses when the verdict counts don't sum to the row count,
which catches the sharper failure: a row with no leading verdict, or two, leaves
a tally that still adds up while no longer counting what it claims to.
Plant-proven on both, using the real 139/220 figures. The `plus N 🚫 premises`
clause below is hand-curated and deliberately outside the check — it names
specific rows, so it is a claim about which, not how many.

## 🚫 wording audit (28 Jul 2026)

Directive: an inherently-unprovable claim must not read as a guarantee — that is
exactly how wp_tunnel's comment became a false safety claim. All nine read against
their code comments:

- **Re-scoped in code** (they overclaimed to a reader): #15 ("any non-530 proves the
  path" — now names the no-forged-HTTP assumption and that a forge can only
  over-claim Live), #18 ("negative-cache no longer possible" — now "compliant
  resolver, RFC 2308; a noncompliant router can still lie, hence the second-device
  check"), #90 ("can never swap bytes silently" — now states it's ghcr's contract,
  untestable, and that the local checksum is what's actually load-bearing), #149
  ("never clash" — now "their stock ports, their defaults not a guarantee;
  `ensure_free` is the actual guard").
- **Already carried their scope**: #17 (self-labeled HONEST LIMIT), #43 (behavior
  proven live; only the ITP attribution is unprovable), #52 (the RFC citation IS the
  scope), #95 (self-flagged UNVERIFIED), #154 (dated, versioned, with its
  falsification case).

## Defect families — the claim and the check aren't looking at the same thing

These shapes have each shipped a false or overstated guard this month. They are one
family: a claim asserts a property of THING X, but the check that "proves" it looks
at THING Y ≠ X. They diverge along different axes:

- **Redundant computation** — one fact computed in two places; the claim is that the
  two agree, and nothing checks that they *can't* diverge (the single-source-of-truth
  rows #174/#177, footer-vs-tab).
- **Time** — a one-time check on a mutable fact; the claim holds for the dependent
  thing's LIFETIME, the check holds ONCE at mint (the share-guard #188 shape; memory
  `one-fact-lifetime-guards`).
- **Coverage / surface** — a guard asserts a property of a whole SURFACE but checks
  one PLACE inside it. Instances this month:
  - `tail_log` docroot leak (#199/#201): "docroot dropped from the view" asserted of
    ALL output; the drop was checked on `list_sites` and re-emitted via log content.
  - the sandbox invariant (`examples/common/mod.rs`): "no example writes real app
    data" asserted of ALL 109 examples; structure covers the ~20 that call
    `sandbox()`, and the bin cache is a real, mutable hole even there. **21 Aug
    2026 — the surface was wider than that sentence too.** 25 examples call
    `sites::provision` without pinning `sites_dir`, so they write into the user's
    own `~/rexenv/Sites`, and SEVEN of them then `remove_dir_all` a docroot
    there. Those seven are now `common::pin_fixture_sites_dir`; the other ~17
    still write real folders and are a row in `docs/TODO.md`. The instructive
    part is that the hole was DOCUMENTED here as a known limit and the sentence
    named the wrong dimension — paths versus ports — while a third dimension (a
    setting read from `$HOME`, reachable from a sandboxed platform) went
    unmentioned for a month.
  - the M1 read-only guard (#199): asserted of the whole read surface; originally
    scanned only `tools.rs` when the boundary is also `ReadCtx` in `readctx.rs`
    (fixed — now scans both, proven to fire on a planted violation).
  - #167's planned rusqlite grep: asserts "core writes no app SQL", but a grep for
    the `rusqlite` import checks IMPORTS, not SQL-string content. (Built 15 Aug
    2026 as the SQL-string scan this note demanded — and its first run found both
    a false claim, feed.rs, and a live violation, commands/mcp.rs.)
- **Data / fixture — a mock or fixture friendlier than production.** The check exercises
  the right surface at the right time, but on UNREPRESENTATIVE inputs, so the gap is in the
  DATA/mock, not the code. THREE misses this session, and the escalation is the point — the
  same shape hid a leak, a cosmetic bug, and a crash:
  - **A leak, unseen:** the secret-leak sweep passed on a debug.log with a *relative* path
    when production logs carry *absolute* stack-trace paths that leak the docroot + OS
    username (the fixture misled the THING verified — it never triggered the leak).
  - **A cosmetic bug, certified fine:** the DevUiReview mock used friendly ids (`s-ea`), so
    the WebKit screenshot showed `→ myblog.test` while production stores a `uuid::new_v4()`
    and rendered `→ 550e8400-e29b-4…` (the fixture misled the VERIFIER).
  - **A crash, masked:** DevUiReview's `mockIPC` returns a canned `McpStatus` for
    `mcp_set_enabled`, so 10 green harness scenarios certified the toggle while the REAL
    command *aborted the packaged app* (`Handle::current` off the runtime, #203). A mock of
    the command under test can hide any backend behaviour — up to a hard crash.
  Each fixed by making the input production-shaped AND baking a probe that fails on the fake
  shape (real UUIDs + a no-bare-UUID probe; a plain-thread bind guard). Memory
  `fixtures-must-look-like-production`; L2's structural blind spot is stated in TESTING.md §1.
  - **A FOURTH, 16 Aug 2026, and the one that defeats the instinct rather than a
    fixture: the unrepresentative input was the machine's own live state, and the
    verification method was "check it by hand."** Deriving which patch a pool runs from
    `ps -o comm=` looks correct on this Mac: the 7.4.33 and 8.0.30 masters print their real
    executable path. Every other pool does not — php-fpm rewrites its process title, so
    8.1–8.4 print `php-fpm: master process (…/config/php-fpm-8.3.conf)`, which names the
    MINOR and never the patch. So an argv implementation passes a hand check on **exactly
    the two pools anyone would poke at first** and is silently wrong for the rest. There is
    no fixture to make production-shaped here — the fixture IS the machine, and it is
    friendlier than production by accident. `lsof -p <pid> -a -d txt` reads the executable
    descriptor, which a title rewrite cannot touch (#342). **The lesson is about the method:
    "verify it by hand" is the instinct this family defeats, because a hand check samples
    whatever is convenient and convenience correlates with the oldest, simplest cases.**

- **A guard that reads a SUPERSET of the thing it claims about** (5 Aug 2026, #235 —
  and the instance is worth keeping because of where it happened). The archive
  button's copy guard scans `RepoPanel.tsx` for the clauses the tooltip must
  contain. Planted the exact edit it exists to stop — both load-bearing clauses
  deleted from the rendered strings — and it **passed**. The comment above those
  constants quotes both phrases while explaining that they are load-bearing, so
  the guard was satisfied by its own explanation. The claim is about **what a user
  sees**; the check read the file, which is a superset containing prose that merely
  *mentions* the words.
  - This is the coverage/surface family with the inclusion reversed. The usual
    instance checks one PLACE inside a wider surface; this one checked a wider
    surface than the claim, which fails in the same direction — the check can be
    true while the claim is false — and is harder to see, because "it scans the
    whole file" sounds more thorough, not less.
  - **Fixed by scanning what renders**: comments stripped, plus two canaries —
    the stripped text must still contain `const ARCHIVE_TITLE` (or every check
    passes on an empty string) and must NOT contain `LOAD-BEARING` (or prose can
    satisfy it again). Both are the same discipline as the `#[cfg(test)]`
    stripper's coverage canary.
  - **The general rule this adds**: any guard that greps a source file for
    user-facing text must exclude the parts of that file which are *about* the
    text. Documentation of a guarantee and the guarantee itself live in the same
    file more often than not — that is usually good practice, and it is exactly
    what makes this failure easy to write.

- **The same family at its largest scale: THE MACHINE WAS THE FIXTURE** (4 Aug 2026,
  ledger #228). The three cases above are a fake string, a fake id and a fake command
  response — each a thing someone WROTE, and so each findable by reading what was
  written. This one had nothing written at all. Asked whether the bundled WP-CLI
  carries `dist-archive`, the check was the obvious one — run it:

  ```
  $ php wp-cli.phar dist-archive --help
  NAME  wp dist-archive          ← yes, apparently
  ```

  It resolved out of `~/.wp-cli/packages/`, installed on this laptop in **December
  2021** and forgotten. The phar has no such command. Had the answer been taken, the
  feature would have shipped a button that worked on **exactly one machine in the
  world**, and the bug report would have read "works here" from the only person who
  could not reproduce it.
  - **Why it is worth its own line.** "Verify on this machine" is the DEFAULT
    instinct, and it is usually the right one — running the thing beats reasoning
    about it nearly always. This is the case where it is actively misleading, and
    the reason is structural: a dev machine is the most contaminated environment
    the software will ever run in, and it is contaminated *precisely* in the
    direction of making things work. Every capability a developer ever installed is
    a fixture nobody chose, nobody reviewed, and nobody can see in a diff.
  - **The tell, and it generalises.** The claim was about what the SHIPPED ARTEFACT
    contains; the check ran the artefact **plus the machine around it**. Same
    claim-vs-check divergence as the rest of the family, but the extra term is
    ambient rather than authored — which is why reading the test would never have
    found it. What found it was asking *where does this answer come from*, then
    removing the suspected source (`WP_CLI_PACKAGES_DIR` at an empty dir) and
    watching the capability disappear.
  - **The procedure that survives this.** For any "does the bundle contain X",
    a positive result on a dev machine is **not evidence** — the only sound form is
    to run it with the ambient sources of X neutralised, and prefer the negative
    control (prove the capability VANISHES when the suspect source is removed) over
    the positive (it works). This is the negative-control instinct the plant-proving
    discipline already uses for guards, pointed at the environment instead of at code.

- **Unguarded prose rots; guarded prose does not — and this project produced the
  controlled experiment for it (3 Aug 2026).** Not "docs drift", which invites a
  shrug and a tidy-up. The finding is sharper and it has a control: **two copies
  of the same guarantee, written by the same author in the same week, describing
  the same system — one of them checked by a test, one not.**
  - The **unchecked** one (`PLAN-mcp-server.md` §6.0) accumulated **three** false
    sentences over three weeks as scratch sites, `wp_run` and the reaper landed:
    "your sites … are not reachable from any tool" (true of mutation; M1's read
    tools reach every site), "share it" as a promotion trigger (removed by #214),
    "nothing the agent runs can write back to your checkout" (true of the clone,
    false of `wp eval`). All three are the §3.1(c) shape.
  - The **checked** one (the Settings card, held by
    `the_enable_moment_copy_says_what_an_agent_can_actually_do`) is honest on all
    three points — because when the first executing tool landed, the guard FAILED
    THE BUILD and the copy was rewritten (#211). It could not rot, so it didn't.
  - **The difference was a test.** Same author, same weeks, same subject; the
    only variable was whether a check existed. Every argument for the
    guard-the-claim discipline before this was a priori — this pair is evidence.
  - Two corollaries worth keeping attached. **(a) The catch generalises past the
    catch:** one sentence was pointed out; the other two came from re-reading the
    rest on the principle that if one claim drifted, others may have. Fixing what
    you were shown is the smaller half of the job. **(b) The right words already
    existed, in the wrong place:** #216's row says "the guarantee is the direction
    rather than the mechanism" — precisely the distinction §6.0 got wrong about
    the checkout. A claim's precision has to live where the claim is READ, not
    only where it was reasoned out; a careful ledger row does not immunise a
    careless paragraph three files away.

- **A diff that aggregates BOTH sides the same way will always agree** (found
  3 Aug 2026, in a verification script rather than in the product — which is
  where this family is hardest to see, because nothing user-facing is wrong).
  Re-verifying `THIRD-PARTY-NOTICES.md` before the release, the check keyed
  each side on crate NAME. Several crates appear in the graph at two versions,
  so both sides collapsed identically and the comparison reconciled at 367/367
  — a clean all-clear from a check that was comparing the wrong thing. It was
  caught only because the count disagreed with the file's own heading (389), and
  the honest instinct was to doubt the heading. Re-keyed on `(name, version)`:
  the true totals are 389/389, and **two npm rows turned out to be malformed** —
  `@tauri-apps/api` and `@tauri-apps/plugin-dialog` had name, version and licence
  collapsed into one cell, so the table rendered 110 rows against a heading
  claiming 112. **The graph was fine; the ARTEFACT was wrong, and it is the
  artefact that ships.** Two lessons: a comparison's key must be as unique as the
  thing being claimed about, and "regenerate before each release" is not
  self-checking — the file had been wrong since the day it was generated and
  three weeks of green gates never looked at it.
  - **And the same file, again, 5 Aug — this time the DIRECTION was wrong.**
    Reconciling npm for the dist-archive release: 112 documented rows against a
    **127-package** production closure. Fifteen packages were undocumented, all
    MIT, several genuinely bundled — `scheduler` (React's), `loose-envify`,
    `js-tokens` and `util-deprecate` are all in `dist/assets/*.js`. Three prior
    passes reported "112/112, nothing added" because each checked only
    `table ⊆ graph`: every row documented does exist, which was true, and
    nothing ever asked whether the graph held rows the table did not.
    **`graph − table` is the direction that finds an omission, and it is the one
    that matters — a notice you never wrote is exactly the one nobody misses.**
    Note the shape difference from the entry above: that one compared the wrong
    KEY, this one compared in one DIRECTION. A set comparison has two ways to be
    vacuously reassuring and this file has now produced both of them.

- **Checks that all compare the same way agree with each other and with nothing
  else** (5 Aug 2026 — the generalisation the notices defect forced, and it is
  worth stating apart from that instance). `THIRD-PARTY-NOTICES.md` was
  reconciled three times, by two different methods, across three weeks. All three
  passes asked `is every row I have real?` and none asked `is every real thing a
  row?`. They agreed — 112/112 each time — and the agreement was the problem: it
  read as corroboration when it was one check run three times. Fifteen bundled
  packages were undocumented throughout.
  - **Repetition is not independence.** Re-running a check, or writing a second
    one that shares the first one's direction, key, or fixture, multiplies
    confidence without adding coverage. That is the most expensive shape here,
    because effort spent on it feels like diligence.
  - **The cheap defence** is to ask what would have to be true for the check to
    pass while the claim is false, and then test THAT: the missing direction, the
    non-unique key, the production-shaped input. Every entry in this section is
    an instance of failing to ask it.
  - Note the family this closes over. Wrong KEY (crates collapsed by name), wrong
    DIRECTION (notices, three times), wrong SURFACE (narrower, and — #235 —
    wider), wrong LIFETIME, wrong DATA. A set comparison has that many ways to be
    vacuously reassuring, and this project has now produced all of them.

**The audit question this adds** — belongs in whatever the audit procedure becomes:
for every guard, *does the check cover the whole surface the claim names, for the
whole lifetime the claim spans, on data shaped like production?* If the claim says
"all X" and the check reads "one X" — or reads X on friendly-fake data — the guard is
narrower than its claim, and that gap (in code OR in the fixture) is exactly where the
next false-safety comment hides.

## The 🔨 backlog, ranked by blast radius

Directive: worked top-down when there's slack — never a session grinding the tail.
Rank = what a FALSE claim costs, not how easy the proof is.

**Tier 1 — cross-site exposure, auth bypass, or data loss if false:**

> Struck entries are closed and kept for the tier's shape. Checked 21 Aug 2026 against
> each row's own verdict, which is the only way this index stays honest: four entries
> were still listed as backlog while their rows read ✅ (#37, #49, #104/#191, and #228
> already struck). `docs/TODO.md` sends an agent here to "work the tiers, top first",
> so a closed row at the top of the list costs the next session its first hour.

## The highest-risk cluster (work these first)

**Row IDs and a hook only — the verdicts live in the rows.** This section used to
restate what each row proved, and that prose was wrong FIVE times out of seven when
finally checked against the rows (13–15 Aug 2026: #103 said "never a live 404" after
`dotfile_guard_check` proved it; #10 said "untested through a live tunnel" the day
after leg 6 tested it; #175 said "untested at any level" over two L0 tests; #36 and
#44 sat listed as open while their rows read ✅ since T10). Each was found the same
way — by working the row it summarised — and the section that carried the
stale-index warning was the thing that kept going stale. The ranking is the only
editorial content here, and row IDs in an order do not drift; everything else is a
lookup.

Open, in order:

1. **#2** — the CF-header watch (a fact about Cloudflare; can go false silently).
2. **#175** (wiring half) — rides SMOKE-TEST's cold-cache login item.

Closed since this list was first ranked — see the rows: #33, #307, #308 (tunnel
replay + ordering), #10, #13 (cross-site + override fallthrough), #103 (dotfiles —
nginx first, and since 15 Aug 2026 genuinely all three backends; this line claimed all three EARLY, the stale-summary shape again), #37, #36, #44; #40/#166 (webview dialogs + custom-scheme
redirect — automatable halves landed at L0+L1, the L2 idea measured impossible,
the eye-only remainder mapped to SMOKE).
